# Audit log — every change, and why

**What this is:** the row-level record of what changed in this Brain, when, and the reason for it. One row per meaningful change. Append-only, newest at the bottom, never rewritten.

**Status:** current — opened 2026-08-31 at Austin's instruction.

## Why this exists

A page tells you what is true now. It does not tell you what it used to say, who changed it, or what they were reasoning from. That gap is where trust in a Brain quietly dies: six weeks from now someone reads a number, disagrees with it, and has no way to find out whether it was corrected, overwritten by mistake, or right all along.

Austin asked for this on 2026-08-31, in the same breath as asking that updates carry notes on why. The two are the same instruction: **a change without a reason attached is not finished.**

## How this works

- **One row per meaningful change.** A new page, a fact replaced, a number corrected, a file moved, a rule added, an item closed. Not typos, not formatting.
- **Newest at the bottom.** Append-only, exactly like `log.md` and `decisions-log.md`. Never rewrite a row. If a change is later reversed, add a new row saying so and reference the old one.
- **The Why column is the point.** Not what changed — the table already says that — but what made the change necessary and what would have happened without it. If the reason is "Austin said so," name the date and quote him.
- **The Affects column is the second point.** Anything that inherits the change: a downstream page, a number, a report, a decision that was made on the old version. This is what makes the log useful when something breaks later.
- **Reversals and corrections get a row of their own,** flagged `REVERSAL` or `CORRECTION` in the What column, referencing the row being undone.

## How this differs from the other three logs

| Log | Grain | Answers |
|---|---|---|
| `00-Meta/log.md` | One entry per session or filing run | What work happened, in narrative |
| `00-Meta/decisions-log.md` | One line per business decision | What the company decided |
| `00-Meta/audit-log.md` | One row per change to this Brain | What changed in here, and why |
| `00-Meta/commitments.md` | One item per open commitment | What is still owed |

`log.md` says "the second vault was folded in." This log says which page each fact landed on and what it displaced. Everything before 2026-08-31 lives only in `log.md`; nothing has been reconstructed backwards, because a fabricated audit row is worse than a missing one.

| 2026-09-03 | **The dash census was overstated and the verification method that produced it was broken. Corrected: the vault holds 7,671 em and en dashes across 527 of 620 files, not the 8,817 across 542 recorded earlier.** Roughly 1,150 of the earlier count were false positives. **Two separate method failures, in opposite directions.** (1) `grep '[emdash][endash]'` in a non UTF-8 locale degrades to a byte class matching `E2`, `80`, `94` and `93`, so it also counts every curly apostrophe (`E2 80 99`) and every middot (`C2 B7`). **The property register rows use the middot as a field separator and the quoted rows are full of curly apostrophes**, so the inflation was concentrated exactly where the counts were highest. (2) `grep -P '\x{2014}'` **errors with "character value too large"** when the locale is unset and returns nothing, which reads identically to a clean file. **The working method is `LC_ALL=C.UTF-8 grep -P`**, and it is now the standard for any dash check in this vault. | **The first method over-counted and the second silently under-counted to zero, and both were used in the same session.** The byte class produced a census that sized the remaining work 15% too large. **The `-P` failure was the dangerous one: it reported the entire vault at zero dashes and every register clean, and a clean report from a broken check is worse than no check.** It was caught only by testing the pattern against a file with a known em dash in it, which is the whole of hard rule 10 in one line: verify the instrument before trusting the reading. **The property agent's claim that both its registers were at zero was correct and was briefly doubted on the strength of a bad measurement.** Its own counts were right throughout. | Every dash count in this file written before this row. **The remaining Path B work is smaller than recorded**, and the standing method for measuring it changes. |
